                [32m'* Wasp exposes every Operation as an HTTP `POST` endpoint on the server at `/operations/<kebab-case-operation-name>`. Authenticated calls send an `Authorization: Bearer <sessionId>` header. The relevant endpoints are therefore:\n'[39m +
                [32m'  * `POST http://localhost:3001/operations/get-board`\n'[39m +
                [32m'  * `POST http://localhost:3001/operations/create-column`\n'[39m +
                [32m'  * `POST http://localhost:3001/operations/create-card`\n'[39m +
                [32m'  * `POST http://localhost:3001/operations/move-card`\n'[39m +
                [32m'* Username authentication endpoints (no session required): `POST http://localhost:3001/auth/username/signup` and `POST http://localhost:3001/auth/username/login`, each accepting a JSON body `{ "username": string, "password": string }`; a successful login responds with a JSON body containing a `sessionId` string.\n'[39m +
                [32m'* Any of the four operations invoked without a valid session must fail with `HttpError` status `401`.\n'[39m +
                [32m"* `getBoard` (no payload) returns the current user's board as a JSON array of column objects ordered by `position` ascending. Each column object must contain at least the keys `id`, `title`, `position`, and `cards`, where `cards` is an array of that column's cards ordered by `position` ascending. Each card object must contain at least the keys `id`, `title`, `position`, and `columnId`.\n"[39m +
                [32m'* `createColumn` (payload keys: `title` string, `position` number) creates a column owned by the current user at the given position and returns the created column object including `id`, `title`, `position`.\n'[39m +
                [32m'* `createCard` (payload keys: `title` string, `columnId` number, `position` number) creates a card with the given title and position inside the column identified by `columnId`, and returns the created card object including `id`, `title`, `position`, `columnId`. If `columnId` does not identify a column owned by the current user, the call must fail with `HttpError` status `403` or `404`.\n'[39m +
                [32m"* `moveCard` (payload keys: `cardId` number, `targetColumnId` number, `afterCardId` number optional, `beforeCardId` number optional) moves the card identified by `cardId` into the column identified by `targetColumnId` and assigns it a new fractional `position` according to the rules below. It updates only the moved card: the card's `columnId` becomes `targetColumnId` and its `position` is recomputed. No other card's `position` may change. It returns the updated card object including `id`, `title`, `position`, `columnId`. `moveCard` does not accept a caller-supplied position; the server computes it.\n"[39m +
                [32m'  * `afterCardId`, when provided, identifies the card that must end up immediately before the moved card (i.e. with a strictly smaller `position`).\n'[39m +
                [32m'  * `beforeCardId`, when provided, identifies the card that must end up immediately after the moved card (i.e. with a strictly larger `position`).\n'[39m +
                [32m"  * When both `afterCardId` and `beforeCardId` are provided, the moved card's new `position` must equal exactly the arithmetic mean of those two neighbors' `position` values.\n"[39m +
                [32m"  * When only `afterCardId` is provided, the moved card is placed at the tail of the target column: its new `position` must be strictly greater than that neighbor's `position`.\n"[39m +
                [32m"  * When only `beforeCardId` is provided, the moved card is placed at the head of the target column: its new `position` must be strictly smaller than that neighbor's `position`.\n"[39m +
                [32m'  * When neither neighbor is provided, the moved card is the only ordering reference in the target column and may take any valid `position`.\n'[39m +
                [32m'  * `cardId` and `targetColumnId` must identify a card and a column owned by the current user. `afterCardId` and `beforeCardId`, when provided, must identify cards that currently belong to the target column and are owned by the current user. Any violation (a missing resource, or a resource owned by another user) must fail with `HttpError` status `403` or `404`.\n'[39m +
                [32m"* The authenticated page at `/` must move a card optimistically: when a move is triggered from the UI, the cached result of the `getBoard` query must be updated to reflect the card's new column and ordering before the server confirms the change, using Wasp's client-side action optimistic-update mechanism for the `moveCard` action."[39m
            }

                      [32m'  id Int @id @default(autoincrement())\n'[39m +

                      [32m'}\n'[39m +

                      [32m`  head: ["<link rel='icon' href='/favicon.ico' />"],\n`[39m +
                      [32m'  auth: {\n'[39m +
                      [32m'    userEntity: "User",\n'[39m +
                      [32m'    methods: {\n'[39m +
                      [32m'      usernameAndPassword: {}, // use this or email, not both\n'[39m +
                      [32m'      email: {}, // use this or usernameAndPassword, not both\n'[39m +
                      [32m'      google: {},\n'[39m +
                      [32m'      gitHub: {},\n'[39m +
                      [32m'    },\n'[39m +
                      [32m'    onAuthFailedRedirectTo: "/someRoute",\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  },\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  // ...\n'[39m +
                      [32m'})\n'[39m +
                      [32m'```\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'Read more about the `auth` field options in the [API Reference](#api-reference) section.\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'We will provide a quick overview of auth in Wasp and link to more detailed documentation for each auth method.\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'## Available auth methods\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'Wasp supports the following auth methods:\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'### [Email »](https://wasp.sh/docs/auth/email)\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'[Email verification, password reset, etc.](https://wasp.sh/docs/auth/email)\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'### [Username & Password »](https://wasp.sh/docs/auth/username-and-pass)\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'[The simplest way to get started](https://wasp.sh/docs/auth/username-and-pass)\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'### [Google »](https://wasp.sh/docs/auth/social-auth/google)\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'[Users sign in with their Google account](https://wasp.sh/docs/auth/social-auth/google)\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'### [Github »](https://wasp.sh/docs/auth/social-auth/github)\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'[Users sign in with their Github account](https://wasp.sh/docs/auth/social-auth/github)\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'### [Keycloak »](https://wasp.sh/docs/auth/social-auth/keycloak)\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'[Users sign in with their Keycloak account](https://wasp.sh/docs/auth/social-auth/keycloak)\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'### [Slack »](https://wasp.sh/docs/auth/social-auth/slack)\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'[Users sign in with their Slack account](https://wasp.sh/docs/auth/social-auth/slack)\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'### [Discord »](https://wasp.sh/docs/auth/social-auth/discord)\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'[Users sign in with their Discord account](https://wasp.sh/docs/auth/social-auth/discord)\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'Click on each auth method for more details.\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"Let's say we enabled the [Username & password](https://wasp.sh/docs/auth/username-and-pass) authentication.\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'We get an auth backend with signup and login endpoints. We also get the `user` object in our [Operations](https://wasp.sh/docs/data-model/operations/overview) and we can decide what to do based on whether the user is logged in or not.\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'We would also get the [Auth UI](https://wasp.sh/docs/auth/ui) generated for us. We can set up our login and signup pages where our users can **create their account** and **login**. We can then protect certain pages by setting `authRequired: true` for them. This will make sure that only logged-in users can access them.\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"We will also have access to the `user` object in our frontend code, so we can show different UI to logged-in and logged-out users. For example, we can show the user's name in the header alongside a **logout button** or a login button if the user is not logged in.\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'## Different ways to use auth\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'When you have decided which auth methods you want to support, you can also choose how you want to present the authorization flows to your users.\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'#### Generated components\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"This is the fastest way to ship, with Wasp generating ready-made components for your app. They allow for some customization to make them consistent with your app. You don't need to implement any UI or logic, and they just work.\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'### [Email »](https://wasp.sh/docs/auth/email)\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'### [Username and password »](https://wasp.sh/docs/auth/username-and-pass)\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'### [Social Auth »](https://wasp.sh/docs/auth/social-auth/overview)\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'#### Make your own UI {#custom-auth-ui}\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'Wasp is flexible enough to let you completely customize your login and signup interface. We give you the auth related functions, and you decide how and when to call them. This allows for total customization of the look-and-feel, and the interaction, but it needs a bit more work.\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'### [Email »](https://wasp.sh/docs/auth/email/create-your-own-ui)\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'### [Username and password »](https://wasp.sh/docs/auth/username-and-pass/create-your-own-ui)\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'### [Social Auth »](https://wasp.sh/docs/auth/social-auth/create-your-own-ui)\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m':::tip\n'[39m +
                      [32m"You don't have to choose one *or* the other! Mix-and-match, and use what you need in each moment. For example, you can create a custom signup screen, but use Wasp's generated components for login.\n"[39m +
                      [32m':::\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'#### Custom login and signup actions\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'The previously discussed options should cover the vast majority of cases. But, for the few instances where it is not enough, you can [create your own signup flows](https://wasp.sh/docs/auth/advanced/custom-auth-actions), with completely custom logic. This is not recommended, and reserved for advanced use cases. Please check first if other Wasp features (mainly [auth hooks](https://wasp.sh/docs/auth/auth-hooks)) can handle your requirements.\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'## Protecting a page with `authRequired`\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'When declaring a page, you can set the `authRequired` property.\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'If you set it to `true`, only authenticated users can access the page. Unauthenticated users are redirected to a route defined by the `auth.onAuthFailedRedirectTo` field.\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'```ts title="main.wasp.ts"\n'[39m +
                      [32m'import { app, page, route } from "@wasp.sh/spec"\n'[39m +
                      [32m'import Main from "./src/pages/Main" with { type: "ref" }\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'export default app({\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  // ...\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  spec: [\n'[39m +
                      [32m'    route("MainRoute", "/", page(Main, { authRequired: true })),\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  ],\n'[39m +
                      [32m'})\n'[39m +
                      [32m'```\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m':::caution[Requires auth method]\n'[39m +
                      [32m'You can only use `authRequired` if your app uses one of the [available auth methods](#available-auth-methods).\n'[39m +
                      [32m':::\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"If `authRequired` is set to `true`, the page's React component (passed as the first argument to `page(...)`) receives the `user` object as a prop. Read more about the `user` object in the [Accessing the logged-in user section](#accessing-the-logged-in-user).\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'## Logout action\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"We provide an action for logging out the user. Here's how you can use it:\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'```tsx title="src/components/LogoutButton.tsx"\n'[39m +
                      [32m'import { logout } from "wasp/client/auth"\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'const LogoutButton = () => {\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  return <button onClick={logout}>Logout</button>\n'[39m +
                      [32m'}\n'[39m +
                      [32m'```\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'## Accessing the logged-in user\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"You can get access to the `user` object both on the server and on the client. The `user` object contains the logged-in user's data.\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'The `user` object has all the fields that you defined in your `User` entity. In addition to that, it will also contain all the auth-related fields that Wasp stores. This includes things like the `username` or the email verification status. For example, if you have a user that signed up using an email and password, the `user` object might look like this:\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'```ts\n'[39m +
                      [32m'const user = {\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  // User data\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  id: "cluqsex9500017cn7i2hwsg17",\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  address: "Some address",\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  // Auth methods specific data\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  identities: {\n'[39m +
                      [32m'    email: {\n'[39m +
                      [32m'      id: "user@app.com",\n'[39m +
                      [32m'      isEmailVerified: true,\n'[39m +
                      [32m'      emailVerificationSentAt: "2024-04-08T10:06:02.204Z",\n'[39m +
                      [32m'      passwordResetSentAt: null,\n'[39m +
                      [32m'    },\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  },\n'[39m +
                      [32m'}\n'[39m +
                      [32m'```\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'You can read more about how the `User` is connected to the rest of the auth system and how you can access the user data in the [Accessing User Data](https://wasp.sh/docs/auth/entities) section of the docs.\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'### On the client\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'There are two ways to access the `user` object on the client:\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'- the `user` prop\n'[39m +
                      [32m'- the `useAuth` hook\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'#### Getting the `user` in authenticated routes\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"If the page's spec sets `authRequired` to `true`, the page's React component receives the `user` object as a prop. This is the simplest way to access the user inside an authenticated page:\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'```ts title="main.wasp.ts"\n'[39m +
                      [32m'import { app, page, route } from "@wasp.sh/spec"\n'[39m +
                      [32m'import Account from "./src/pages/Account" with { type: "ref" }\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'export default app({\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  // ...\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  spec: [\n'[39m +
                      [32m'    route("AccountRoute", "/account", page(Account, {\n'[39m +
                      [32m'      authRequired: true\n'[39m +
                      [32m'    })),\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  ],\n'[39m +
                      [32m'})\n'[39m +
                      [32m'```\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'```tsx title="src/pages/Account.tsx"\n'[39m +
                      [32m'import type { AuthUser } from "wasp/auth";\n'[39m +
                      [32m'import Button from "./Button";\n'[39m +
                      [32m'import { logout } from "wasp/client/auth";\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'const AccountPage = ({ user }: { user: AuthUser }) => {\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  return (\n'[39m +
                      [32m'    <div>\n'[39m +
                      [32m'      <Button onClick={logout}>Logout</Button>\n'[39m +
                      [32m'      {JSON.stringify(user, null, 2)}\n'[39m +
                      [32m'    </div>\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  );\n'[39m +
                      [32m'};\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'export default AccountPage;\n'[39m +
                      [32m'```\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'#### Getting the `user` in non-authenticated routes\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'Wasp provides a React hook you can use in the client components - `useAuth`.\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"This hook is a thin wrapper over Wasp's `useQuery` hook and returns data in the same format.\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'```tsx title="src/pages/MainPage.tsx"\n'[39m +
                      [32m'import { useAuth, logout } from "wasp/client/auth";\n'[39m +
                      [32m'import { Link } from "react-router";\n'[39m +
                      [32m'import Todo from "../Todo";\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'export function Main() {\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  const { data: user } = useAuth();\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  if (!user) {\n'[39m +
                      [32m'    return (\n'[39m +
                      [32m'      <span>\n'[39m +
                      [32m'        Please <Link to="/login">login</Link> or{" "}\n'[39m +
                      [32m'        <Link to="/signup">sign up</Link>.\n'[39m +
                      [32m'      </span>\n'[39m +
                      [32m'    );\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  } else {\n'[39m +
                      [32m'    return (\n'[39m +
                      [32m'      <>\n'[39m +
                      [32m'        <button onClick={logout}>Logout</button>\n'[39m +
                      [32m'        <Todo />\n'[39m +
                      [32m'      </>\n'[39m +
                      [32m'    );\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  }\n'[39m +
                      [32m'}\n'[39m +
                      [32m'```\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'### On the server\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'#### Using the `context.user` object\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"When authentication is enabled, all [queries and actions](https://wasp.sh/docs/data-model/operations/overview) have access to the `user` object through the `context` argument. `context.user` contains all User entity's fields and the auth identities connected to the user. We strip out the `hashedPassword` field from the identities for security reasons.\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'```ts title="src/actions.ts"\n'[39m +
                      [32m'import type { Task } from "wasp/entities";\n'[39m +
                      [32m'import type { CreateTask } from "wasp/server/operations";\n'[39m +

                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'type CreateTaskPayload = Pick<Task, "description">;\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'export const createTask: CreateTask<CreateTaskPayload, Task> = async (\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  args,\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  context,\n'[39m +

                      [32m'  if (!context.user) {\n'[39m +

                      [32m'  }\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  const Task = context.entities.Task;\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  return Task.create({\n'[39m +
                      [32m'    data: {\n'[39m +
                      [32m'      description: args.description,\n'[39m +
                      [32m'      user: {\n'[39m +
                      [32m'        con'[39m... 15233 more characters
                  }
                ]

                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'# Queries\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"We'll explain what Queries are and how to use them. If you're looking for a detailed API specification, skip ahead to the [API Reference](#api-reference).\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"You can use Queries to fetch data from the server. They shouldn't modify the server's state. Fetching all comments on a blog post, a list of users that liked a video, information about a single product based on its ID... All of these are perfect use cases for a Query.\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m':::tip\n'[39m +
                      [32m"Queries are fairly similar to Actions in terms of their API. Therefore, if you're already familiar with Actions, you might find reading the entire guide repetitive.\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'We instead recommend skipping ahead and only reading [the differences between Queries and Actions](https://wasp.sh/docs/data-model/operations/actions#differences-between-queries-and-actions), and consulting the [API Reference](#api-reference) as needed.\n'[39m +
                      [32m':::\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'## Working with Queries\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"You declare queries in the Wasp file and implement them using NodeJS. Wasp not only runs these queries within the server's context but also creates code that enables you to call them from any part of your codebase, whether it's on the client or server side.\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"This means you don't have to build an HTTP API for your query, manage server-side request handling, or even deal with client-side response handling and caching. Instead, just concentrate on implementing the business logic inside your query, and let Wasp handle the rest!\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'To create a Query, you must:\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'1. Declare the Query in Wasp using the `query` spec.\n'[39m +
                      [32m"2. Define the Query's NodeJS implementation.\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"After completing these two steps, you'll be able to use the Query from any point in your code.\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'### Specifying Queries\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'To create a Query in Wasp, we begin with a `query` spec.\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"Let's declare two Queries - one to fetch all tasks, and another to fetch tasks based on a filter, such as whether a task is done:\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'```ts title="main.wasp.ts"\n'[39m +
                      [32m'import { app, query } from "@wasp.sh/spec"\n'[39m +
                      [32m'import { getAllTasks, getFilteredTasks } from "./src/queries" with { type: "ref" }\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'export default app({\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  // ...\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  spec: [\n'[39m +
                      [32m'    query(getAllTasks),\n'[39m +
                      [32m'    query(getFilteredTasks),\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  ],\n'[39m +
                      [32m'})\n'[39m +
                      [32m'```\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'If you want to know about all supported options for the `query` spec, take a look at the [API Reference](#api-reference).\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m':::note\n'[39m +
                      [32m'When `main.wasp.ts` needs to point to your code, it uses imports like this:\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'```ts\n'[39m +
                      [32m'import { MainPage } from "./src/MainPage" with { type: "ref" }\n'[39m +
                      [32m'```\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'Notice the `with { type: "ref" }` part at the end of the import statement. This tells Wasp to treat the import as a reference to your app\'s code, without running the imported code. For more details and examples, see [reference imports](https://wasp.sh/docs/general/spec#reference-imports).\n'[39m +
                      [32m':::\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m':::info\n'[39m +
                      [32m"You might have noticed that we told Wasp to import Query implementations that don't yet exist. Don't worry about that for now. We'll write the implementations imported from `queries.ts` in the next section.\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"It's a good idea to start with the high-level concept (the Query spec in the Wasp file) and only then deal with the implementation details (the Query's implementation in JavaScript).\n"[39m +
                      [32m':::\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"After declaring a Wasp Query, Wasp derives the Query's name from the function you pass to `query`. For example, `query(getFilteredTasks)` creates a Query named `getFilteredTasks`.\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'Two important things then happen:\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"- Wasp **generates a server-side NodeJS function** with the Query's name.\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"- Wasp **generates a client-side JavaScript function** with the Query's name (e.g., `getFilteredTasks`). This function takes a single optional argument - an object containing any serializable data you wish to use inside the Query. Wasp will send this object over the network and pass it into the Query's implementation as its first positional argument (more on this when we look at the implementations). Such an abstraction works thanks to an HTTP API route handler Wasp generates on the server, which calls the Query's NodeJS implementation under the hood.\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'Generating these two functions ensures a similar calling interface across the entire app (both client and server).\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'### Implementing Queries in Node\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"Now that we've declared the Query, what remains is to implement it. We've instructed Wasp to look for the Queries' implementations in the file `src/queries.ts`, so that's where we should export them from.\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"Here's how you might implement the previously declared Queries `getAllTasks` and `getFilteredTasks`:\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'```ts title="src/queries.ts"\n'[39m +
                      [32m'import { type GetAllTasks, type GetFilteredTasks } from "wasp/server/operations"\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'type Task = {\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  id: number\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  description: string\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  isDone: boolean\n'[39m +
                      [32m'}\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'// our "database"\n'[39m +
                      [32m'const tasks: Task[] = [\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  { id: 1, description: "Buy some eggs", isDone: true },\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  { id: 2, description: "Make an omelette", isDone: false },\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  { id: 3, description: "Eat breakfast", isDone: false },\n'[39m +
                      [32m']\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"// You don't need to use the arguments if you don't need them\n"[39m +
                      [32m'export const getAllTasks: GetAllTasks<void, Task[]> = () => {\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  return tasks\n'[39m +
                      [32m'}\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"// The 'args' object is something sent by the caller (most often from the client)\n"[39m +
                      [32m'export const getFilteredTasks: GetFilteredTasks<\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  Pick<Task, "isDone">,\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  Task[]\n'[39m +
                      [32m'> = (args) => {\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  const { isDone } = args\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  return tasks.filter((task) => task.isDone === isDone)\n'[39m +
                      [32m'}\n'[39m +
                      [32m'```\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m':::info[Payload constraints]\n'[39m +
                      [32m"Wasp uses [superjson](https://github.com/flightcontrolhq/superjson) under the hood. This means you're not limited to only sending and receiving JSON payloads.\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'Wasp will automatically handle the serialization and deserialization [for all the data types that superjson supports](https://github.com/flightcontrolhq/superjson#decimaljs--prismadecimal:~\\:text=Superjson%20supports%20many%20extra%20types) (like `biging`, `Date`, `Map`, `Set`, etc.), and for [Prisma.Decimal](https://www.prisma.io/docs/orm/prisma-client/special-fields-and-types#working-with-decimal).\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"As long as you're annotating your Operations with the correct automatically generated types, TypeScript ensures your payloads are valid (i.e., Wasp knows how to serialize and deserialize them).\n"[39m +
                      [32m':::\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'#### Type support for Queries\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"Wasp automatically generates the types `GetAllTasks` and `GetFilteredTasks` based on your Wasp file's specs:\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'- `GetAllTasks` is a generic type automatically generated by Wasp, based on the Query spec for `getAllTasks`.\n'[39m +
                      [32m'- `GetFilteredTasks` is also a generic type automatically generated by Wasp, based on the Query spec for `getFilteredTasks`.\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"Use these types to type the Query's implementation. It's optional but very helpful since doing so properly types the Query's context.\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'In this case, TypeScript will know the `context.entities` object must include the `Task` entity. TypeScript also knows whether the `context` object includes user information (it depends on whether your Query uses auth).\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'The generated types are generic and accept two optional type arguments: `Input` and `Output`.\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'1. `Input` - The argument (the payload) received by the Query function.\n'[39m +
                      [32m"2. `Output` - The Query function's return type.\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"Use these type arguments to type the Query's inputs and outputs.\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'Explanation for the example above\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"The above code says that the Query `getAllTasks` doesn't expect any arguments (its input type is `void`), but it does return a list of tasks (its output type is `Task[]`).\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'On the other hand, the Query `getFilteredTasks` expects an object of type `{ isDone: boolean }`. This type is derived from the `Task` entity type.\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"If you don't care about typing the Query's inputs and outputs, you can omit both type arguments. TypeScript will then infer the most general types (`never` for the input and `unknown` for the output).\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'Specifying `Input` or `Output` is completely optional, but we highly recommended it. Doing so gives you:\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'- Type support for the arguments and the return value inside the implementation.\n'[39m +
                      [32m"- **Full-stack type safety**. We'll explore what this means when we discuss calling the Query from the client.\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'Read more about type support for implementing Queries in the [API Reference](#implementing-queries).\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m':::tip[Inferring the return type]\n'[39m +
                      [32m"If don't want to explicitly type the Query's return value, the `satisfies` keyword tells TypeScript to infer it automatically:\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'```typescript\n'[39m +
                      [32m'const getFoo = (async (_args, context) => {\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  const foos = await context.entities.Foo.findMany()\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  return {\n'[39m +
                      [32m'    foos,\n'[39m +
                      [32m'    message: "Here are some foos!",\n'[39m +
                      [32m'    queriedAt: new Date(),\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  }\n'[39m +
                      [32m'}) satisfies GetFoo\n'[39m +
                      [32m'```\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'From the snippet above, TypeScript knows:\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'1. The correct type for `context`.\n'[39m +
                      [32m"2. The Query's return type is `{ foos: Foo[], message: string, queriedAt: Date }`.\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"If you don't need the context, you can skip specifying the Query's type (and arguments):\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'```typescript\n'[39m +
                      [32m'const getFoo = () => ({ name: "Foo", date: new Date() })\n'[39m +
                      [32m'```\n'[39m +
                      [32m':::\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'For a detailed explanation of the Query definition API (more precisely, its arguments and return values), check the [API Reference](#api-reference).\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'### Using Queries\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'#### Using Queries on the client\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'To call a Query on the client, you can import it from `wasp/client/operations` and call it directly.\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"The usage doesn't change depending on whether the Query is authenticated or not. Wasp authenticates the logged-in user in the background.\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'```ts\n'[39m +
                      [32m'import { getAllTasks, getFilteredTasks } from "wasp/client/operations"\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'// TypeScript automatically infers the return values and type-checks\n'[39m +
                      [32m'// the payloads.\n'[39m +
                      [32m'const allTasks = await getAllTasks()\n'[39m +
                      [32m'const doneTasks = await getFilteredTasks({ isDone: true })\n'[39m +
                      [32m'```\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"Wasp supports **automatic full-stack type safety**. You only need to specify the Query's type in its server-side definition, and the client code will automatically know its API payload types.\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'#### Using Queries on the server\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'Calling a Query on the server is similar to calling it on the client.\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"Here's what you have to do differently:\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'- Import Queries from `wasp/server/operations` instead of `wasp/client/op'[39m... 9385 more characters
                  }
                ]

                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'# Actions\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"We'll explain what Actions are and how to use them. If you're looking for a detailed API specification, skip ahead to the [API Reference](#api-reference).\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"Actions are quite similar to [Queries](https://wasp.sh/docs/data-model/operations/queries), but with a key distinction: Actions are designed to modify and add data, while Queries are solely for reading data. Examples of Actions include adding a comment to a blog post, liking a video, or updating a product's price.\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'Actions and Queries work together to keep data caches up-to-date.\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m':::tip\n'[39m +
                      [32m"Actions are almost identical to Queries in terms of their API. Therefore, if you're already familiar with Queries, you might find reading the entire guide repetitive.\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'We instead recommend skipping ahead and only reading [the differences between Queries and Actions](#differences-between-queries-and-actions), and consulting the [API Reference](#api-reference) as needed.\n'[39m +
                      [32m':::\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'## Working with Actions\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"Actions are declared in Wasp and implemented in NodeJS. Wasp runs Actions within the server's context, but it also generates code that allows you to call them from anywhere in your code (either client or server) using the same interface.\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"This means you don't have to worry about building an HTTP API for the Action, managing server-side request handling, or even dealing with client-side response handling and caching. Instead, just focus on developing the business logic inside your Action, and let Wasp handle the rest!\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'To create an Action, you need to:\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'1. Declare the Action in Wasp using the `action` spec.\n'[39m +
                      [32m"2. Implement the Action's NodeJS functionality.\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'Once these two steps are completed, you can use the Action from anywhere in your code.\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'### Specifying Actions\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"To create an Action in Wasp, we begin with an `action` spec. Let's declare two Actions - one for creating a task, and another for marking tasks as done:\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'```ts title="main.wasp.ts"\n'[39m +
                      [32m'import { action, app } from "@wasp.sh/spec"\n'[39m +
                      [32m'import { createTask, markTaskAsDone } from "./src/actions" with { type: "ref" }\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'export default app({\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  // ...\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  spec: [\n'[39m +
                      [32m'    action(createTask),\n'[39m +
                      [32m'    action(markTaskAsDone),\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  ],\n'[39m +
                      [32m'})\n'[39m +
                      [32m'```\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'If you want to know about all supported options for the `action` spec, take a look at the [API Reference](#api-reference).\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m':::note\n'[39m +
                      [32m'When `main.wasp.ts` needs to point to your code, it uses imports like this:\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'```ts\n'[39m +
                      [32m'import { MainPage } from "./src/MainPage" with { type: "ref" }\n'[39m +
                      [32m'```\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'Notice the `with { type: "ref" }` part at the end of the import statement. This tells Wasp to treat the import as a reference to your app\'s code, without running the imported code. For more details and examples, see [reference imports](https://wasp.sh/docs/general/spec#reference-imports).\n'[39m +
                      [32m':::\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m':::info\n'[39m +
                      [32m"You might have noticed that we told Wasp to import Action implementations that don't yet exist. Don't worry about that for now. We'll write the implementations imported from `actions.ts` in the next section.\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"It's a good idea to start with the high-level concept (the Action spec in the Wasp file) and only then deal with the implementation details (the Action's implementation in JavaScript).\n"[39m +
                      [32m':::\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"After declaring a Wasp Action, Wasp derives the Action's name from the function you pass to `action`. For example, `action(markTaskAsDone)` creates an Action named `markTaskAsDone`.\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'Two important things then happen:\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"- Wasp **generates a server-side NodeJS function** with the Action's name.\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"- Wasp **generates a client-side JavaScript function** with the Action's name (e.g., `markTaskAsDone`). This function takes a single optional argument - an object containing any serializable data you wish to use inside the Action. Wasp will send this object over the network and pass it into the Action's implementation as its first positional argument (more on this when we look at the implementations). Such an abstraction works thanks to an HTTP API route handler Wasp generates on the server, which calls the Action's NodeJS implementation under the hood.\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'Generating these two functions ensures a similar calling interface across the entire app (both client and server).\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'### Implementing Actions in Node\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"Now that we've declared the Action, what remains is to implement it. We've instructed Wasp to look for the Actions' implementations in the file `src/actions.ts`, so that's where we should export them from.\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"Here's how you might implement the previously declared Actions `createTask` and `markTaskAsDone`:\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'```ts title="src/actions.ts"\n'[39m +
                      [32m'import { type CreateTask, type MarkTaskAsDone } from "wasp/server/operations"\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'type Task = {\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  id: number\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  description: string\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  isDone: boolean\n'[39m +
                      [32m'}\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'// our "database"\n'[39m +
                      [32m'let nextId = 4\n'[39m +
                      [32m'const tasks = [\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  { id: 1, description: "Buy some eggs", isDone: true },\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  { id: 2, description: "Make an omelette", isDone: false },\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  { id: 3, description: "Eat breakfast", isDone: false },\n'[39m +
                      [32m']\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"// You don't need to use the arguments if you don't need them\n"[39m +
                      [32m'export const createTask: CreateTask<Pick<Task, "description">, Task> = (\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  args\n'[39m +
                      [32m') => {\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  const newTask = {\n'[39m +
                      [32m'    id: nextId,\n'[39m +
                      [32m'    isDone: false,\n'[39m +
                      [32m'    description: args.description,\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  }\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  nextId += 1\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  tasks.push(newTask)\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  return newTask\n'[39m +
                      [32m'}\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"// The 'args' object is something sent by the caller (most often from the client)\n"[39m +
                      [32m'export const markTaskAsDone: MarkTaskAsDone<Pick<Task, "id">, void> = (\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  args\n'[39m +
                      [32m') => {\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  const task = tasks.find((task) => task.id === args.id)\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  if (!task) {\n'[39m +
                      [32m"    // We'll show how to properly handle such errors later\n"[39m +
                      [32m'    return\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  }\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  task.isDone = true\n'[39m +
                      [32m'}\n'[39m +
                      [32m'```\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m':::info[Payload constraints]\n'[39m +
                      [32m"Wasp uses [superjson](https://github.com/flightcontrolhq/superjson) under the hood. This means you're not limited to only sending and receiving JSON payloads.\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'Wasp will automatically handle the serialization and deserialization [for all the data types that superjson supports](https://github.com/flightcontrolhq/superjson#decimaljs--prismadecimal:~\\:text=Superjson%20supports%20many%20extra%20types) (like `biging`, `Date`, `Map`, `Set`, etc.), and for [Prisma.Decimal](https://www.prisma.io/docs/orm/prisma-client/special-fields-and-types#working-with-decimal).\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"As long as you're annotating your Operations with the correct automatically generated types, TypeScript ensures your payloads are valid (i.e., Wasp knows how to serialize and deserialize them).\n"[39m +
                      [32m':::\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'#### Type support for Actions\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'Wasp automatically generates the types `CreateTask` and `MarkTaskAsDone` based on the specs in your Wasp file:\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'- `CreateTask` is a generic type that Wasp automatically generated based on the Action spec for `createTask`.\n'[39m +
                      [32m'- `MarkTaskAsDone` is a generic type that Wasp automatically generated based on the Action spec for `markTaskAsDone`.\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"Use these types to type the Action's implementation. It's optional but very helpful since doing so properly types the Action's context.\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'In this case, TypeScript will know the `context.entities` object must include the `Task` entity. TypeScript also knows whether the `context` object includes user information (it depends on whether your Action uses auth).\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'The generated types are generic and accept two optional type arguments: `Input` and `Output`.\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'1. `Input` - The argument (the payload) received by the Action function.\n'[39m +
                      [32m"2. `Output` - The Action function's return type.\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"Use these type arguments to type the Action's inputs and outputs.\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'Explanation for the example above\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"The above code says that the Action `createTask` expects an object with the new task's description (its input type is `Pick<Task, 'description'>`) and returns the new task (its output type is `Task`).\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"On the other hand, the Action `markTaskAsDone` expects an object of type `Pick<Task, 'id'>`. This type is derived from the `Task` entity type.\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"If you don't care about typing the Action's inputs and outputs, you can omit both type arguments. TypeScript will then infer the most general types (`never` for the input and `unknown` for the output).\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'Specifying `Input` or `Output` is completely optional, but we highly recommended it. Doing so gives you:\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'- Type support for the arguments and the return value inside the implementation.\n'[39m +
                      [32m"- **Full-stack type safety**. We'll explore what this means when we discuss calling the Action from the client.\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'Read more about type support for implementing Actions in the [API Reference](#implementing-actions).\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m':::tip[Inferring the return type]\n'[39m +
                      [32m"If don't want to explicitly type the Action's return value, the `satisfies` keyword tells TypeScript to infer it automatically:\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'```typescript\n'[39m +
                      [32m'const createFoo = (async (_args, context) => {\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  const foo = await context.entities.Foo.create()\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  return {\n'[39m +
                      [32m'    newFoo: foo,\n'[39m +
                      [32m`    message: "Here's your foo!",\n`[39m +
                      [32m'    returnedAt: new Date(),\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  }\n'[39m +
                      [32m'}) satisfies CreateFoo\n'[39m +
                      [32m'```\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'From the snippet above, TypeScript knows:\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'1. The correct type for `context`.\n'[39m +
                      [32m"2. The Action's return type is `{ newFoo: Foo, message: string, returnedAt: Date }`.\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"If you don't need the context, you can skip specifying the Action's type (and arguments):\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'```typescript\n'[39m +
                      [32m'const createFoo = () => ({ name: "Foo", date: new Date() })\n'[39m +
                      [32m'```\n'[39m +
                      [32m':::\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'For a detailed explanation of the Action definition API (more precisely, its arguments and return values), check the [API Reference](#api-reference).\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'### Using Actions\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'#### Using Actions on the client\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'To call an Action on the client, you can import it from `wasp/client/operations` and call it directly.\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"The usage doesn't depend on whether the Action is authenticated or not. Wasp authenticates the logged-in user in the background.\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'```ts\n'[39m +
                      [32m'import { createTask, markTaskAsDone } from "wasp/client/operations"\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'// TypeScript automatically infers the return values and type-checks\n'[39m +
                      [32m'// the payloads.\n'[39m +
                      [32m'const newTask = await createTask({ description: "Keep learning TypeScript" })\n'[39m +
                      [32m'await markTaskAsDone({ id: 1 })\n'[39m +
                      [32m'```\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"Wasp supports **automatic full-stack type safety**. You only need to specify the Action's type in it"[39m... 15694 more characters

                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"If you support multiple auth methods, you'll need to find which identity exists for the user and then access its data:\n"[39m +

                      [32m'if (user.identities.email !== null) {\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  const email = user.identities.email.id\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  // ...\n'[39m +
                      [32m'} else if (user.identities.google !== null) {\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  const googleId = user.identities.google.id\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  // ...\n'[39m +
                      [32m'}\n'[39m +
                      [32m'```\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'### `getFirstProviderUserId` Helper\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'The `getFirstProviderUserId` method returns the first user ID that it finds for the user. For example if the user has signed up with email, it will return the email. If the user has signed up with Google, it will return the Google ID.\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'This can be useful if you support multiple authentication methods and you need *any* ID that identifies the user in your app.\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'```tsx title="src/MainPage.tsx"\n'[39m +
                      [32m'import { type AuthUser } from "wasp/auth"\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'const MainPage = ({ user }: { user: AuthUser }) => {\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  const userId = user.getFirstProviderUserId()\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  // ...\n'[39m +
                      [32m'}\n'[39m +
                      [32m'```\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'```ts title="src/tasks.ts"\n'[39m +
                      [32m'export const createTask: CreateTask<...>  = async (args, context) => {\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  const userId = context.user.getFirstProviderUserId()\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  // ...\n'[39m +
                      [32m'}\n'[39m +
                      [32m'```\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\\* Multiple identities per user will be possible in the future and then the `getFirstProviderUserId` method will return the ID of the first identity that it finds without any guarantees about which one it will be.\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'## Including the User with Other Entities\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"Sometimes, you might want to include the user's data when fetching other entities. For example, you might want to include the user's data with the tasks they have created.\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"We'll mention the `auth` and the `identities` relations which we will explain in more detail later in the [Entities Explained](#entities-explained) section.\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m':::caution[Be careful about sensitive data]\n'[39m +
                      [32m"You'll need to include the `auth` and the `identities` relations to get the full auth data about the user. However, you should keep in mind that the `providerData` field in the `identities` can contain sensitive data like the user's hashed password (in case of email or username auth), so you will likely want to exclude it if you are returning those values to the client.\n"[39m +
                      [32m':::\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"You can include the full user's data with other entities using the `include` option in the Prisma queries:\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'```ts title="src/tasks.ts"\n'[39m +
                      [32m'export const getAllTasks = (async (args, context) => {\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  return context.entities.Task.findMany({\n'[39m +
                      [32m'    orderBy: { id: "desc" },\n'[39m +
                      [32m'    select: {\n'[39m +
                      [32m'      id: true,\n'[39m +
                      [32m'      title: true,\n'[39m +
                      [32m'      user: {\n'[39m +
                      [32m'        include: {\n'[39m +
                      [32m'          auth: {\n'[39m +
                      [32m'            include: {\n'[39m +
                      [32m'              identities: {\n'[39m +
                      [32m'                // Including only the `providerName` and `providerUserId` fields\n'[39m +
                      [32m'                select: {\n'[39m +
                      [32m'                  providerName: true,\n'[39m +
                      [32m'                  providerUserId: true,\n'[39m +
                      [32m'                },\n'[39m +
                      [32m'              },\n'[39m +
                      [32m'            },\n'[39m +
                      [32m'          },\n'[39m +
                      [32m'        },\n'[39m +
                      [32m'      },\n'[39m +
                      [32m'    },\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  })\n'[39m +
                      [32m'}) satisfies tasks.GetAllQuery<{}, {}>\n'[39m +
                      [32m'```\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"If you have some **piece of the auth data that you want to access frequently** (for example the `username`), it's best to store it at the top level of the `User` entity.\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"For example, save the `username` or `email` as a property on the `User` and you'll be able to access it without including the `auth` and `identities` fields. We show an example in the [Defining Extra Fields on the User Entity](https://wasp.sh/docs/auth/overview#1-defining-extra-fields) section of the docs.\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'### Getting Auth Data from the User Object\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'When you have the `user` object with the `auth` and `identities` fields, it can be a bit tedious to obtain the auth data (like username or Google ID) from it:\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'```tsx title="src/MainPage.tsx"\n'[39m +
                      [32m'function MainPage() {\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  // ...\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  return (\n'[39m +
                      [32m'    <div className="tasks">\n'[39m +
                      [32m'      {tasks.map((task) => (\n'[39m +
                      [32m'        <div key={task.id} className="task">\n'[39m +
                      [32m'          {task.title} by {task.user.auth?.identities[0].providerUserId}\n'[39m +
                      [32m'        </div>\n'[39m +
                      [32m'      ))}\n'[39m +
                      [32m'    </div>\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  )\n'[39m +
                      [32m'}\n'[39m +
                      [32m'```\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"Wasp offers a few helper methods to access the user's auth data when you retrieve the `user` like this. They are `getUsername`, `getEmail` and `getFirstProviderUserId`. They can be used both on the client and the server.\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'#### `getUsername`\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'It accepts the `user` object and if the user signed up with the [Username & password](https://wasp.sh/docs/auth/username-and-pass) auth method, it returns the username or `null` otherwise. The `user` object needs to have the `auth` and the `identities` relations included.\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'```tsx title="src/MainPage.tsx"\n'[39m +
                      [32m'import { getUsername } from "wasp/auth"\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'function MainPage() {\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  // ...\n'[39m +
                      [32m'  return (\n'[39m +
                      [32m'    <div className="tasks">\n'[39m +
                      [32m'      {tasks.map((task) => (\n'[39m +

            [32m'- When staging files, prefer adding specific files by name rather than using "git add -A" or "git add .", which can accidentally include sensitive files (.env, credentials) or large binaries\n'[39m +

                [32m'\n'[39m +
                [32m'## Background\n'[39m +
                [32m'\n'[39m +
                [32m'Build a full-stack Kanban board application with the Wasp framework using the TypeScript spec (`main.wasp.ts`, `wasp: { version: "^0.24.0" }`). The board is made of columns (statuses) and cards. Cards can be moved within a column and across columns. Ordering is stored as a fractional floating-point `position`, so moving a card only ever updates that single card\'s `position` and never renumbers its siblings.\n'[39m +
                [32m'\n'[39m +
                [32m'The app uses username & password authentication, a SQLite database (the Wasp development default), Prisma for the data model, and Wasp Operations for the API. All board data is private to the authenticated user who owns it.\n'[39m +
                [32m'\n'[39m +
                [32m'## Requirements\n'[39m +
                [32m'\n'[39m +
                [32m'* Data model (`schema.prisma`):\n'[39m +
                [32m'  * `Column`: `id` (Int, primary key), `title` (String), `position` (Float), and an owner relation to `User`.\n'[39m +
                [32m'  * `Card`: `id` (Int, primary key), `title` (String), `position` (Float), a relation to its `Column` exposed through a `columnId` (Int) foreign key, and an owner relation to `User`.\n'[39m +
                [32m'  * The `User` entity required by Wasp auth.\n'[39m +
                [32m'* Enable username & password authentication. Every operation below must act only on data owned by the currently authenticated user.\n'[39m +
                [32m'* Implement the following Wasp Operations. The operation names must match exactly (Wasp derives the HTTP route from the name):\n'[39m +
                [32m'  * Query `getBoard`\n'[39m +
                [32m'  * Action `createColumn`\n'[39m +
                [32m'  * Action `createCard`\n'[39m +
                [32m'  * Action `moveCard`\n'[39m +
                [32m'* Provide a root route `/` that renders an authenticated page displaying the board and performing an optimistic move (see the optimistic-update requirement below).\n'[39m +
                [32m'\n'[39m +
                [32m'## Implementation Hints\n'[39m +
                [32m'\n'[39m +
                [32m'* Project path: /home/user/kanban\n'[39m +
                [32m'* Start command (run from the project path): `wasp start`\n'[39m +
                [32m'* The Wasp server listens on port 3001; the web client listens on port 3000.\n'[39m +
                [32m'* Wasp exposes every Operation as an HTTP `POST` endpoint on the server at `/operations/<kebab-case-operation-name>`. Authenticated calls send an `Authorization: Bearer <sessionId>` header. The relevant endpoints are therefore:\n'[39m +
                [32m'  * `POST http://localhost:3001/operations/get-board`\n'[39m +
                [32m'  * `POST http://localhost:3001/operations/create-column`\n'[39m +
                [32m'  * `POST http://localhost:3001/operations/create-card`\n'[39m +
                [32m'  * `POST http://localhost:3001/operations/move-card`\n'[39m +
                [32m'* Username authentication endpoints (no session required): `POST http://localhost:3001/auth/username/signup` and `POST http://localhost:3001/auth/username/login`, each accepting a JSON body `{ "username": string, "password": string }`; a successful login responds with a JSON body containing a `sessionId` string.\n'[39m +
                [32m'* Any of the four operations invoked without a valid session must fail with `HttpError` status `401`.\n'[39m +
                [32m"* `getBoard` (no payload) returns the current user's board as a JSON array of column objects ordered by `position` ascending. Each column object must contain at least the keys `id`, `title`, `position`, and `cards`, where `cards` is an array of that column's cards ordered by `position` ascending. Each card object must contain at least the keys `id`, `title`, `position`, and `columnId`.\n"[39m +
                [32m'* `createColumn` (payload keys: `title` string, `position` number) creates a column owned by the current user at the given position and returns the created column object including `id`, `title`, `position`.\n'[39m +
                [32m'* `createCard` (payload keys: `title` string, `columnId` number, `position` number) creates a card with the given title and position inside the column identified by `columnId`, and returns the created card object including `id`, `title`, `position`, `columnId`. If `columnId` does not identify a column owned by the current user, the call must fail with `HttpError` status `403` or `404`.\n'[39m +
                [32m"* `moveCard` (payload keys: `cardId` number, `targetColumnId` number, `afterCardId` number optional, `beforeCardId` number optional) moves the card identified by `cardId` into the column identified by `targetColumnId` and assigns it a new fractional `position` according to the rules below. It updates only the moved card: the card's `columnId` becomes `targetColumnId` and its `position` is recomputed. No other card's `position` may change. It returns the updated card object including `id`, `title`, `position`, `columnId`. `moveCard` does not accept a caller-supplied position; the server computes it.\n"[39m +
                [32m'  * `afterCardId`, when provided, identifies the card that must end up immediately before the moved card (i.e. with a strictly smaller `position`).\n'[39m +
                [32m'  * `beforeCardId`, when provided, identifies the card that must end up immediately after the moved card (i.e. with a strictly larger `position`).\n'[39m +
                [32m"  * When both `afterCardId` and `beforeCardId` are provided, the moved card's new `position` must equal exactly the arithmetic mean of those two neighbors' `position` values.\n"[39m +
                [32m"  * When only `afterCardId` is provided, the moved card is placed at the tail of the target column: its new `position` must be strictly greater than that neighbor's `position`.\n"[39m +
                [32m"  * When only `beforeCardId` is provided, the moved card is placed at the head of the target column: its new `position` must be strictly smaller than that neighbor's `position`.\n"[39m +
                [32m'  * When neither neighbor is provided, the moved card is the only ordering reference in the target column and may take any valid `position`.\n'[39m +
                [32m'  * `cardId` and `targetColumnId` must identify a card and a column owned by the current user. `afterCardId` and `beforeCardId`, when provided, must identify cards that currently belong to the target column and are owned by the current user. Any violation (a missing resource, or a resource owned by another user) must fail with `HttpError` status `403` or `404`.\n'[39m +
                [32m"* The authenticated page at `/` must move a card optimistically: when a move is triggered from the UI, the cached result of the `getBoard` query must be updated to reflect the card's new column and ordering before the server confirms the change, using Wasp's client-side action optimistic-update mechanism for the `moveCard` action."[39m

                      [32m'import { app } from "@wasp.sh/spec"\n'[39m +

                      [32m'}\n'[39m +

                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"Account merging means that multiple auth identities can be merged into a single user account.                       [32m'\n'[39m +

                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'#### Generated components\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m"This is the fastest way to ship, with Wasp generating ready-made components for your app. They allow for some customization to make them consistent with your app. You don't need to implement any UI or logic, and they just work.\n"[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'### [Email »](https://wasp.sh/docs/auth/email)\n'[39m +

                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'Wasp is flexible enough to let you completely customize your login and signup interface. We give you the auth related functions, and you decide how and when to call them. This allows for total customization of the look-and-feel, and the interaction, but it needs a bit more work.\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +
                      [32m'### [Email »](https://wasp.sh/docs/auth/email/create-your-own-ui)\n'[39m +
                      [32m'\n'[39m +

                      [32m'\n'[39m +

                      [32m"You don't have to choose one *or* the other! Mix-and-match, and use what you need in each moment. For example, you can create a custom signup screen, but use Wasp's generated components for login.\n"[39m +
